SUBjECT :              REPORT 011 UNIDENTIFIED OB.ECT


      D, A, F, I ,

      1,        The Duty Officer at Headquarters, Training Command,
      last night rang the officer on duty here at Deportment of Air,
      who made the following entry in the log book,
                     2055K Call from Duty Officer T. C. re report by
                           tw. Sheeran (librarian,FJ5241) 470 Lygon
                           Str. Carlton (thru W/C Ingledew) of the
                           sighting of a strange object of reddish
                           appearance flying very high in a northerly
                           or north- easterly direction.
      2,        The cell was apparently made to H. Q. T. C. by W/C
      Ingledew, on behalf of Mr. Sheeran.

 '!le llX Sir:
       I Pave sighted for ~vo f i fths to one half second at
 23 . ~5 hours toni ;;ht an object some mi l es distant while
  sitting on a bench near the King ' s Park monument and
  lookin ~ at the centr e of the Ci ty of Perth .
        "'he ol· ject appeared be~·1 een the two red eigne to
 aircraft in a slightly higher level and quite close to
 the red light on my left , and seemed to be approximately
 three tines larger than the overall gl~1ing diameter of
 the red li ~ht , or approxiwately one eighth of an inch .
        In t~e first tenth of a second , the object was
 stationary , coloured deep r ed except for a black spot
 in its upper part and tno smaller black spots in its lo~est
 part , i t s overall scape bein; round to oval .
        In a bout the second tenth of a second , the object
 s t arted to rise almost vertically , with about fifteen
 degrees inclination off a verti cal line to the right , and
 sti l l was coloured deep red .
        In about the t~ird tenth of a second , the object was
 proceeding in the same direction, ~ing speed a little ,
 and changing its upper part to an ~ orange colour , the
 l~1er part remaining deep red .
       In about the fourth tenth of a second , th~on of the
object turned bright orgnge , and i n the next liii•?JQ&r
fraction of a second , the whole object, having gained
speed considerably , disappeared instantly .
       The object travelled in the approximately half second
under review a distance which appeared equivalent to about
three quarters of the apparent distance be~1een the two
red signal lights , in the di rection specified afor e .
       I heard no noise ~hi c~ould be ascribed to the object ,
but the wind blew -.-about rom the oppoei te direction . I
saw no flashes which might ~ ~-est a turbojet or rocket
eru;ine .
      The obj ect appeared similar to the design of a monstrosity
elab orat ed by }erman t echnicians near Prague in the last
months of oVvrld 7~.,_H~ .t Altho~h all rele•1ant files and
re s earch e :tuipment~ ~aken over by the r.:vn in !!ay 1945 ,
                    - 2 -
the director of __Je Aerodyna.mic Institute, Prague University,
Professor Dr. Pesek , sho·.7cd me a f~w sketches 'lfhich he kept
as souvenirs . In his opinion , they indi cated all too clearly
that :iermane wont altogether mad in the end . The monstrosity
was supoosed to attain speeds of over 10,000 km en hour, end
was presumed to have been intended to be p~nered by some
revolutionary engines of an undisclosed type. I recall that
"heavy water" was supposed to have had something to do with
the en,:;ines, Vlh!ltever that may mean .
     Shortly before my departure from Czechoslovakia in 19~8 ,
I understood that Stalin made references, possibly jocular,
to new Soviet craft which he said looked like a little flat
fish . It has now occurred to me that the whole damn thing
may not have been quite so mad or quits so funny as it had
appeared in the years past .
     Altho~~h this information may be of no significance at
all , I am not qualified to judge whether that is the case ~
and so am passing it over to you in strict confidence . I m
sorry for having expressed it in such a clumsy way , but
having majored in ~orld History , Psychology and Political
Science, I am not a technical man .
     Actually , I don ' t suppose the Russians would have passed
any infor~ation regarding that ~erman monstrosity over to
the West, and it is not icpossible t hat only few people
have ever known about it . I still remember a few more
things about it , but it all seems to be too long ago to
make it of any interest now .

             Reference is made to your memoranda Nos. 3295, 3401 and
  3516 on your file 6/A1r(3A) and the following suggestion is offered
  as a possible solution to the myste ry or the sightings.

              Since the objects sighted in all cases seem to have been
  "hovering" and elowl y receding SJway from the obeervere to the west-
  ward
  or   on clear
     pl.,nets    nights, it seems likely that they may have been stars
              setting.

            In ISUpport or this theory, 1t 1s noted that durtnp:
 Auguet the planet Venus appears at about 2800 and about 450 above
 the horizon at 1900 hours and moves to about 2650 and 100 above the
 horizon by 2100 hours , and ie brighter than let m~Jgnitude. It would
 disappear over the horizon South of Wes t at abOll t 2200 boars but may
 not be visible right up to that time .

            The S tar Arcturus would appear as a let magnitude star
 slightly west of N.W. at 10° above tbe horizon at 2100 hours, and
 has a reddish appearance. This may have been the light referred
 to in the latter par t of Pars. 3 of your letter or 25th August.
           At 0300 the planet Ysr~ would appear as a lst megnitude
plqnet of reddish colour just setting on a bearing 2400 from tbe
observer and may have been lila light seen by \Irs. Hay on 23rd August.
           Even though the nights were relatively clear, the presence
of a alight amount or dust haze would give a reddish appearance to
stars and planets close to the horizon, and this would be
accentuated if there were an inverelon present even though stars
overhead were clearly visible as white li gb te. The planet ~ars
an~ the otar Arcturus have a slightly reddish appearance ln a clear
atmosphere. Both pl anets and the star referred to ere QUite
bri~ht objects and the planet Venus would have been the brightest
object in the heavens apart from the moon.

            It is emph asised that the above is only one possible
solution based on th" evidence avallable, and 1f the opportunit•1
offers , you may care to diecuss this aspect with the observers .
If further evidence 'a brought forward whlch would appear t o rule
out this poaaibillty, we would be glad t o hear of it.

       2.     This report was first made verbally by telephone con-
       versation with Sqn.Ldr. Bain,I(s)o. of School o~ Radio, Ball-
       arat on the 13th October, 1954.
      3.     Meteorolosical Sections at Essendon and Laverton were
      contacted immediately by the St~~ Of~icer (Intell. ) at this
      Headquarters.   It wae advised that the time o~ sighting
      (1707 hours) did not co-incide with the times of release of
      meteorological balloons on the date of sighting, and because
      the existing winds were South- West, it is improbable that the
      object sighted was a met. balloon.
     4.     It was further advised, however, that although improb-
     able, it is remotely possible that the object was a met.
     balloon.

      s.                 It ie considered that all possible action baa been
     t~en regarding this sighting and the relevant information
     hae been filed for future reference.


                                                        ~
                                                          (c.w. PEARCE)
                                                          Group Captain,
     ENCL.·                                               For Air Officer OommancUng.

      REPORT OF UNUSUAL LIGHT SI GHTED BY MR . HAY
            OF NO. 1 NEW STREE T TOOWOOMBA.

1.        At 0945 hours , 9 th August 1954 , Mr. Hay of No . 1
New Street, Toowoomba rang to report having seen an
unexplained phenomenon on the evening of Sunday Stb August.
2.         Mr. Hay lives near Mount Kynock and at 2040 hours
be observed a str ange light whi ch appe ared to be hovering a t
a height of 2 000 to 3 0 00 feet in a direction south of west of
hie point of observati on . As far as be coul d judge the l ight
woul d be in the vic i nity of Oakey.
3.           He first observed the l ight when looking from a
wind ow and went outs i de to observe it . His wife also saw
the light . He examined the phenome non thr ough binoculars
but apart from i ntensifying tbe l ight no further detail
was r e veal ed by such examinati ons .
 4.        The l ight appeared to be hovering and t ilting t o
 reveal an upper surface and a l ower surface . Tbe upper
 surface appear ed to be a bright flowing r ed whilst the
 lower surfac e was a bright yel lowish- white . Nr. Bays
 would venture no opinion on the sbape of the object.
 5.         At 2 045 hours the light receded in a dir ec ti on
 south of west unti l it disappeared.
            Mr . Ray is prepare d t o give any furt her infor mation
 6.
 required .

 1.         Under a reciprocal agreement for the exchange of
 information on the above subject, the attached paper,
 purporting to explain inter alia Biblical miracles, flying
 saucers and why airmen ' s families are mainly daughters, has
 been forwarded to this office by the Regional Director,
 Victorian Tasmanian Region(Department of Civil AViation.
 The author of this thesis 1s Mr . c . v; . BE&CHING, 206 Alma Rd.,
 St. Kilda.
 ~.        The paper was considered by the Air Staff of this
 Headquarters but it was unanimously agreed that the thesis
 went beyond the comprehension of the normal G.D. officer.
 3.        The Command Education Officer who is well qualified
 as a physicist, but whose knowledge of the latest developments
 in the popula r science fiction field is limited, considered
 the theory novel and unsupported by any evidence of pure or
 applied science known to him.    He considers that the writer
 has a certain amount of scientific knowledge and appears to
 have read extensively of those scientific publications available
 to the general public.
4.        The ntheo-scientific " nature of the paper ha s been
noted but no approach has been made to the Chaplain ' s Branch
of this Headquarters for their comments.
5.        Unless proper consideration can be given to such
papers, the R.A.A.F. is laying itself open to charges of
cavalier trea tment of persons who genuinely believe that
their contributions Will materially assist in the advancement
of aviation science .   The extreme view co~d be t&ken that this
He~dquarters is as qualified to consider the validity of this
theory as were the 16th century monks in the case of Galilee.
6.        ThQ.refore, in view of t he limited resources of this
Headquarters, the attached paper is forwarded for your
information and action.    It is understood from Department of
Civil Aviation that t his is the only copy in existence and
that ~r. Beaching would like it r eturned eventually ,

          It is desired to thank you for the account of your

any reports received in the fUture.
